View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the modular multiplicative inverse of `a` modulo `n`, which means finding `x` such that:
`a * x ≡ 1 (mod n)`

This is only possible when `a` and `n` are coprime (gcd(a,n) = 1). If they're not coprime, we return None.

## Mathematical Background

The key to solving this is the Extended Euclidean Algorithm, which finds integers x and y such that:
`a * x + n * y = gcd(a, n)`

When gcd(a,n) = 1, we have:
`a * x + n * y = 1`

This means `a * x ≡ 1 (mod n)`, so `x` is our modular inverse.

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Check if a and n are coprime**: Calculate gcd(a,n). If gcd ≠ 1, return None.
2. **Use Extended Euclidean Algorithm**: Find x and y such that a*x + n*y = gcd(a,n)
3. **Extract the inverse**: When gcd = 1, x is our answer, but we need to ensure it's positive and within range [0, n-1]

## Implementation Details

The Extended Euclidean Algorithm works as follows:
- If n = 0, then gcd(a,n) = a, and we return (1, 0)
- Otherwise, recursively solve for (n, a % n), then back-substitute

## Code Implementation

```python
def inverseMod(a, n):
    """
    Find the modular multiplicative inverse of a modulo n.
    Returns x such that (a * x) % n == 1.
    Returns None if no inverse exists (a and n are not coprime).
    """
    
    def extended_gcd(a, b):
        """
        Returns (g, x, y) such that a*x + b*y = g = gcd(a, b)
        """
        if b == 0:
            return a, 1, 0
        else:
            g, x1, y1 = extended_gcd(b, a % b)
            x = y1
            y = x1 - (a // b) * y1
            return g, x, y
    
    # Find gcd and the coefficients
    gcd, x, y = extended_gcd(a, n)
    
    # If gcd is not 1, no modular inverse exists
    if gcd != 1:
        return None
    
    # Make sure the result is positive and within range [0, n-1]
    # x might be negative, so we adjust it
    inverse = x % n
    
    return inverse

# Read input from stdin and solve
a = int(input().strip())
n = int(input().strip())

result = inverseMod(a, n)
print(result)
```

## Verification with Examples

Let's trace through a simple example:
- Find inverse of 3 modulo 11
- We need 3*x ≡ 1 (mod 11)
- Extended Euclidean: 11 = 3*3 + 2, 3 = 2*1 + 1, 2 = 1*2 + 0
- Back substitution: 1 = 3 - 2*1 = 3 - (11 - 3*3)*1 = 3 - 11 + 3*3 = 4*3 - 1*11
- So 4*3 ≡ 1 (mod 11), thus inverse is 4

The algorithm correctly finds this. The modulo operation ensures the result is positive and in the required range.

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the modular multiplicative inverse of `a` modulo `n`, which means finding `x` such that:
`a * x ≡ 1 (mod n)`

This is only possible when `a` and `n` are coprime (their GCD is 1). If they're not coprime, we return None.

## Mathematical Approach

The most efficient way to find the modular multiplicative inverse is using the **Extended Euclidean Algorithm**.

The Extended Euclidean Algorithm finds integers `x` and `y` such that:
`a * x + n * y = gcd(a, n)`

When `gcd(a, n) = 1`, this gives us `a * x ≡ 1 (mod n)`, so `x` is our answer (we can return `x % n` to ensure it's positive).

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Check if a and n are coprime**: Calculate gcd(a, n). If gcd ≠ 1, return None.

2. **Use Extended Euclidean Algorithm**: 
   - Apply the algorithm to find integers x and y such that `a * x + n * y = gcd(a, n)`
   - When gcd = 1, x is our modular inverse (we need to ensure it's positive)

3. **Handle negative results**: If x is negative, convert it to a positive equivalent by adding n.

## Implementation

```python
def inverseMod(a, n):
    """
    Find the modular multiplicative inverse of a modulo n.
    
    Args:
        a: integer
        n: integer
        
    Returns:
        x such that (a * x) % n == 1, or None if no inverse exists
    """
    def extended_gcd(a, b):
        """Returns (gcd, x, y) such that a*x + b*y = gcd(a, b)"""
        if a == 0:
            return b, 0, 1
        gcd, x1, y1 = extended_gcd(b % a, a)
        x = y1 - (b // a) * x1
        y = x1
        return gcd, x, y
    
    # Find gcd and the coefficients
    gcd, x, y = extended_gcd(a, n)
    
    # If a and n are not coprime, no inverse exists
    if gcd != 1:
        return None
    
    # The modular inverse is x (mod n)
    # Make sure it's positive
    return (x % n + n) % n

# Read input from stdin
a = int(input().strip())
n = int(input().strip())

# Calculate and output the result
result = inverseMod(a, n)
print(result)
```

## How it works:

1. **Extended GCD Function**: Recursively finds coefficients x and y such that `a*x + b*y = gcd(a,b)`

2. **Check coprimality**: If gcd(a,n) ≠ 1, no modular inverse exists

3. **Modular adjustment**: For the solution x, we use `(x % n + n) % n` to ensure the result is positive and in the range [0, n-1]

## Example verification:
- For a=3, n=11: We need x such that 3*x ≡ 1 (mod 11)
- 3*4 = 12 ≡ 1 (mod 11), so x=4
- extended_gcd(3,11) gives gcd=1, x=-4 (since 3*(-4) + 11*2 = 1)
- (-4 % 11) = 7, then (7 + 11) % 11 = 8? Wait, let me recalculate...

Actually, let me verify: 3*(-4) + 11*2 = -12 + 22 = 10, not 1. Let me fix this.

Actually, the extended Euclidean algorithm should give us 3*x + 11*y = 1. Let's trace through:
- gcd(3,11): 11 = 3*3 + 2, 3 = 2*1 + 1, 2 = 1*2 + 0
- Backtracking: 1 = 3 - 2*1 = 3 - (11 - 3*3)*1 = 3 - 11 + 3*3 = 4*3 - 1*11
- So x=4, y=-1

So 3*4 ≡ 1 (mod 11), which is correct. The algorithm works.

The implementation above correctly returns 4 for a=3, n=11.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the modular multiplicative inverse of `a` modulo `n`, which means finding `x` such that:
`a * x ≡ 1 (mod n)`

This is only possible when `a` and `n` are coprime (their GCD is 1). If they're not coprime, we return None.

## Mathematical Approach

The most efficient way to find the modular multiplicative inverse is using the Extended Euclidean Algorithm. This algorithm finds integers x and y such that:
`a * x + n * y = gcd(a, n)`

When `gcd(a, n) = 1`, we get `a * x + n * y = 1`, which means `a * x ≡ 1 (mod n)`. So `x` is our answer (we need to ensure x is positive, so we adjust if necessary).

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Check if gcd(a, n) = 1**: If not, return None
2. **Apply Extended Euclidean Algorithm**: Find x and y such that a*x + n*y = gcd(a, n)
3. **Handle the sign**: If x is negative, convert it to a positive equivalent by adding n

## Implementation Details

The Extended Euclidean Algorithm works recursively:
- Base case: if n = 0, then gcd(a, 0) = a, and we return (1, 0)
- Otherwise: recursively solve for (n, a % n), then express the result in terms of a and n

Let me trace through an example:
- Find inverse of 3 modulo 11
- We need 3x ≡ 1 (mod 11)
- Using Extended Euclidean: 11 = 3×3 + 2, 3 = 2×1 + 1, 2 = 1×2 + 0
- Back-substitute: 1 = 3 - 2×1 = 3 - (11 - 3×3)×1 = 3×4 - 11×1
- So 3×4 ≡ 1 (mod 11), answer is 4

```python
def extended_gcd(a, b):
    """Return (g, x, y) such that a*x + b*y = g = gcd(a, b)"""
    if b == 0:
        return a, 1, 0
    else:
        g, x1, y1 = extended_gcd(b, a % b)
        x = y1
        y = x1 - (a // b) * y1
        return g, x, y

def inverseMod(a, n):
    """Find the modular multiplicative inverse of a modulo n"""
    # First check if they are coprime
    g, x, y = extended_gcd(a, n)
    
    # If gcd is not 1, no inverse exists
    if g != 1:
        return None
    
    # x is the coefficient we found, but it might be negative
    # We need to make it positive modulo n
    inverse = x % n
    
    return inverse

# Read input from stdin
a = int(input().strip())
n = int(input().strip())

# Calculate and output the result
```

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the modular multiplicative inverse of `a` modulo `n`, which means finding `x` such that:
`a * x ≡ 1 (mod n)`

This is only possible when `a` and `n` are coprime (their GCD is 1). If they're not coprime, we return None.

## Mathematical Approach

The most efficient way to find the modular inverse is using the Extended Euclidean Algorithm, which finds solutions to the equation:
`a * x + n * y = gcd(a, n)`

When `gcd(a, n) = 1`, we get `a * x + n * y = 1`, which means `a * x ≡ 1 (mod n)`.

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Check if a and n are coprime**: Calculate gcd(a, n). If gcd ≠ 1, return None.

2. **Use Extended Euclidean Algorithm**: Find integers x and y such that `a * x + n * y = gcd(a, n)`.

3. **Extract the modular inverse**: If gcd = 1, then x is our answer. We need to ensure it's positive (between 0 and n-1).

4. **Handle negative results**: If x is negative, convert it to a positive equivalent by adding n.

## Implementation

```python
def inverseMod(a, n):
    """
    Find the modular multiplicative inverse of a modulo n.
    
    Returns x such that (a * x) % n == 1, or None if no inverse exists.
    """
    
    def extended_gcd(a, b):
        """
        Extended Euclidean Algorithm to find gcd(a, b) and coefficients x, y
        such that a*x + b*y = gcd(a, b)
        """
        if a == 0:
            return b, 0, 1
        gcd, x1, y1 = extended_gcd(b % a, a)
        x = y1 - (b // a) * x1
        y = x1
        return gcd, x, y
    
    # Find gcd and coefficients
    gcd, x, y = extended_gcd(a, n)
    
    # If gcd is not 1, no modular inverse exists
    if gcd != 1:
        return None
    
    # Ensure the result is positive and in the range [0, n-1]
    # x might be negative, so we convert it to its positive equivalent
    inverse = x % n
    
    return inverse

# Read input and solve
a = int(input().strip())
n = int(input().strip())

result = inverseMod(a, n)
print(result)
```

## How it works:

1. **Extended GCD**: The function `extended_gcd(a, b)` returns the GCD and coefficients x, y such that `a*x + b*y = gcd(a, b)`.

2. **Check coprimality**: If the GCD is not 1, there's no modular inverse.

3. **Modular reduction**: The coefficient x from the extended GCD gives us a solution, but it might be negative. We use `% n` to convert it to a positive value in the range [0, n-1].

4. **Input handling**: The program reads two integers from stdin and outputs the result.

## Example verification:
- For a=3, n=11: We want x such that 3*x ≡ 1 (mod 11)
- 3*4 = 12 ≡ 1 (mod 11), so answer should be 4
- extended_gcd(3,11) will find coefficients where 3*x + 11*y = 1
- It will find x=4, y=-1, so 3*4 + 11*(-1) = 12 - 11 = 1 ✓

The algorithm correctly handles this case and returns 4.

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
def inverseMod(a, n):
    """
    Find the modular multiplicative inverse of a modulo n.
    
    Returns x such that (a * x) % n == 1, or None if no inverse exists.
    """
    
    def extended_gcd(a, b):
        """
        Extended Euclidean Algorithm to find gcd(a, b) and coefficients x, y
        such that a*x + b*y = gcd(a, b)
        """
        if a == 0:
            return b, 0, 1
        gcd, x1, y1 = extended_gcd(b % a, a)
        x = y1 - (b // a) * x1
        y = x1
        return gcd, x, y
    
    # Find gcd and coefficients
    gcd, x, y = extended_gcd(a, n)
    
    # If gcd is not 1, no modular inverse exists
    if gcd != 1:
        return None
    
    # Ensure the result is positive and in the range [0, n-1]
    # x might be negative, so we convert it to its positive equivalent
    inverse = x % n
    
    return inverse

# Read input and solve
a = int(input().strip())
n = int(input().strip())

result = inverseMod(a, n)
print(result)

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the modular multiplicative inverse of `a` modulo `n`, which means finding `x` such that:
`a * x ≡ 1 (mod n)`

This is only possible when `a` and `n` are coprime (their GCD is 1). If they're not coprime, we return `None`.

## Algorithm Choice

The most efficient way to find the modular inverse is using the **Extended Euclidean Algorithm**. This algorithm finds integers `x` and `y` such that:
`a * x + n * y = gcd(a, n)`

When `gcd(a, n) = 1`, we have `a * x ≡ 1 (mod n)`, so `x` is our answer (we can take `x % n` to ensure it's positive).

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Check if a and n are coprime**: Calculate GCD of a and n. If GCD ≠ 1, return None.

2. **Apply Extended Euclidean Algorithm**: Find coefficients x and y such that a*x + n*y = gcd(a,n).

3. **Extract the modular inverse**: When gcd = 1, x is the modular inverse. Since we want the positive value in the range [1, n-1], we take x % n.

4. **Handle negative results**: If x is negative, convert it to a positive equivalent by adding n and taking modulo n.

## Implementation

```python
def inverseMod(a, n):
    """
    Find the modular multiplicative inverse of a modulo n.
    
    Args:
        a: integer (positive)
        n: integer (positive)
    
    Returns:
        x such that (a * x) % n == 1, or None if no inverse exists
    """
    def extended_gcd(a, b):
        """Returns (gcd, x, y) such that a*x + b*y = gcd(a, b)"""
        if a == 0:
            return b, 0, 1
        gcd, x1, y1 = extended_gcd(b % a, a)
        x = y1 - (b // a) * x1
        y = x1
        return gcd, x, y
    
    # Find gcd and coefficients
    gcd, x, y = extended_gcd(a, n)
    
    # If gcd is not 1, no modular inverse exists
    if gcd != 1:
        return None
    
    # Ensure the result is positive and in the range [1, n-1]
    # x might be negative, so we convert to positive equivalent
    inverse = x % n
    
    return inverse

# Read input from stdin and solve
a = int(input().strip())
n = int(input().strip())

result = inverseMod(a, n)
print(result)
```

## Verification with Examples

Let's trace through a simple example: a=3, n=11
- We want x such that 3*x ≡ 1 (mod 11)
- GCD(3,11) = 1, so inverse exists
- Extended GCD: 3*x + 11*y = 1
- Working through: 11 = 3*3 + 2, 3 = 2*1 + 1, 2 = 1*2 + 0
- Back-substituting: 1 = 3 - 2*1 = 3 - (11 - 3*3)*1 = 3 - 11 + 3*3 = 4*3 - 1*11
- So x = 4, y = -1
- 3*4 = 12 ≡ 1 (mod 11) ✓

The algorithm correctly finds that the inverse of 3 mod 11 is 4.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the modular multiplicative inverse of `a` modulo `n`, which means finding `x` such that:
`a * x ≡ 1 (mod n)`

This is only possible when `a` and `n` are coprime (their GCD is 1). If they're not coprime, we return None.

## Mathematical Background

The solution uses the Extended Euclidean Algorithm, which finds integers `x` and `y` such that:
`a * x + n * y = gcd(a, n)`

When `gcd(a, n) = 1`, we get:
`a * x + n * y = 1`

This means `a * x ≡ 1 (mod n)`, so `x` is our modular inverse.

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Check if a and n are coprime**: Calculate gcd(a, n). If gcd ≠ 1, return None.

2. **Apply Extended Euclidean Algorithm**: Find x and y such that a*x + n*y = gcd(a, n)

3. **Extract the modular inverse**: When gcd = 1, x is our answer. However, we need to ensure x is positive and in the range [0, n-1].

4. **Handle negative results**: If x is negative, we convert it to its positive equivalent by adding n.

## Implementation

```python
def extended_gcd(a, b):
    """Returns (gcd, x, y) such that a*x + b*y = gcd"""
    if a == 0:
        return b, 0, 1
    gcd, x1, y1 = extended_gcd(b % a, a)
    x = y1 - (b // a) * x1
    y = x1
    return gcd, x, y

def inverseMod(a, n):
    """
    Find the modular multiplicative inverse of a modulo n
    Returns x such that (a * x) % n = 1
    Returns None if no inverse exists (a and n are not coprime)
    """
    # Find gcd and coefficients using extended Euclidean algorithm
    gcd, x, y = extended_gcd(a, n)
    
    # If gcd is not 1, no inverse exists
    if gcd != 1:
        return None
    
    # x is the modular inverse, but it might be negative
    # We need to make it positive and in the range [0, n-1]
    modular_inverse = x % n
    
    return modular_inverse

# Read input from stdin
a = int(input().strip())
n = int(input().strip())

# Calculate and output the result
result = inverseMod(a, n)
print(result)
```

## How it works:

1. The `extended_gcd` function recursively finds coefficients x and y such that a*x + b*y = gcd(a,b)

2. For our problem, when gcd(a,n) = 1, we get a*x + n*y = 1, so a*x ≡ 1 (mod n)

3. The value of x from the extended Euclidean algorithm is our modular inverse, but it might be negative

4. We use `x % n` to convert any negative result to its positive equivalent in the range [0, n-1]

## Example:
- For a=3, n=11: We need to find x such that 3*x ≡ 1 (mod 11)
- Using extended Euclidean algorithm: 3*(-4) + 11*1 = 1
- So x = -4, but -4 mod 11 = 7
- Check: 3*7 = 21 ≡ 1 (mod 11) ✓

The algorithm correctly finds the modular inverse.

`a * x ≡ 1 (mod n)`

This is only possible when `a` and `n` are coprime (their GCD is 1). If they're not coprime, we return None.

## Mathematical Background

The solution uses the Extended Euclidean Algorithm, which finds integers `x` and `y` such that:
`a * x + n * y = gcd(a, n)`

When `gcd(a, n) = 1`, we get:
`a * x + n * y = 1`
which means `a * x ≡ 1 (mod n)`

So `x` is our modular inverse.

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Check if a and n are coprime**: Calculate gcd(a, n). If gcd ≠ 1, return None.

2. **Apply Extended Euclidean Algorithm**: This finds x and y such that a*x + n*y = gcd(a, n).

3. **Extract the modular inverse**: When gcd = 1, x is the modular inverse (but it might be negative, so we convert to positive modulo n).

4. **Handle negative results**: If x is negative, convert it to a positive value in the range [0, n-1].

## Implementation

```python
def inverseMod(a, n):
    """
    Find the modular multiplicative inverse of a modulo n.
    
    Returns x such that (a * x) % n = 1.
    Returns None if no inverse exists (i.e., gcd(a, n) != 1).
    """
    
    def extended_gcd(a, b):
        """
        Extended Euclidean Algorithm to find gcd(a, b) and coefficients x, y
        such that a*x + b*y = gcd(a, b)
        """
        if a == 0:
            return b, 0, 1
        gcd, x1, y1 = extended_gcd(b % a, a)
        x = y1 - (b // a) * x1
        y = x1
        return gcd, x, y
    
    # Find gcd and coefficients
    gcd, x, y = extended_gcd(a, n)
    
    # If a and n are not coprime, no inverse exists
    if gcd != 1:
        return None
    
    # x is the modular inverse, but it might be negative
    # Convert to positive modulo n
    inverse = x % n
    
    return inverse

# Read input and solve
try:
    a = int(input().strip())
    n = int(input().strip())
    result = inverseMod(a, n)
    print(result)
except EOFError:
    pass
```

## How it works with an example:

For a=3, n=11:
- We need to find x such that 3x ≡ 1 (mod 11)
- Extended GCD of 3 and 11 gives us coefficients where 3x + 11y = 1
- The algorithm finds x = 4, y = -1 since 3*4 + 11*(-1) = 12 - 11 = 1
- So 3*4 ≡ 1 (mod 11), thus 4 is the inverse
- 4 % 11 = 4, which is the correct answer

The algorithm correctly handles the negative case and ensures the result is in the range [0, n-1].

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the modular multiplicative inverse of `a` modulo `n`, which means finding `x` such that:
`a * x ≡ 1 (mod n)`

This is only possible when `a` and `n` are coprime (their GCD is 1). If they're not coprime, we return None.

## Mathematical Approach

The most efficient way to find the modular multiplicative inverse is using the **Extended Euclidean Algorithm**.

The Extended Euclidean Algorithm finds integers `x` and `y` such that:
`a * x + n * y = gcd(a, n)`

When `gcd(a, n) = 1`, we have:
`a * x + n * y = 1`

This means `a * x ≡ 1 (mod n)`, so `x` is the modular multiplicative inverse of `a` modulo `n`.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. First, check if `gcd(a, n) == 1`. If not, return None.
2. Use Extended Euclidean Algorithm to find coefficients `x` and `y` such that `a*x + n*y = gcd(a, n)`.
3. Since we only need the modular inverse, we take `x` modulo `n` to ensure it's positive.

## Implementation Details

The Extended Euclidean Algorithm works by:
- If `n = 0`, then `gcd(a, 0) = a`, and we return `(a, 1, 0)`
- Otherwise, recursively solve for `(n, a % n)` and then use the result to find the coefficients for `(a, n)`

## Code Implementation

```python
def extended_gcd(a, b):
    """
    Returns (gcd, x, y) such that a*x + b*y = gcd(a, b)
    """
    if b == 0:
        return a, 1, 0
    else:
        gcd, x1, y1 = extended_gcd(b, a % b)
        x = y1
        y = x1 - (a // b) * y1
        return gcd, x, y

def inverseMod(a, n):
    """
    Find the modular multiplicative inverse of a modulo n.
    Returns x such that (a * x) % n = 1.
    Returns None if no inverse exists (i.e., gcd(a, n) != 1).
    """
    # Find gcd and coefficients
    gcd, x, y = extended_gcd(a, n)
    
    # If gcd is not 1, no inverse exists
    if gcd != 1:
        return None
    
    # Ensure the result is positive by taking modulo n
    # x might be negative, so we convert to positive equivalent
    return (x % n + n) % n

# Read input from stdin
try:
    a = int(input())
    n = int(input())
    result = inverseMod(a, n)
    print(result)
except EOFError:
    pass
```

## How it works with an example:

For a=3, n=11:
- We want to find x such that 3*x ≡ 1 (mod 11)
- Using extended GCD: 3*x + 11*y = 1
- Through the algorithm, we find x = 4, y = -1
- Check: 3*4 = 12 ≡ 1 (mod 11) ✓
- So inverseMod(3, 11) returns 4

The algorithm correctly handles the case where the GCD is not 1 by returning None. The modulo operation ensures the result is positive.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There is an infinite 2-dimensional grid. The robot stands in cell (0, 0) and wants to reach cell (x, y). Here is a list of possible commands the robot can execute:

  * move north from cell (i, j) to (i, j + 1); 
  * move east from cell (i, j) to (i + 1, j); 
  * move south from cell (i, j) to (i, j - 1); 
  * move west from cell (i, j) to (i - 1, j); 
  * stay in cell (i, j). 



The robot wants to reach cell (x, y) in as few commands as possible. However, he can't execute the same command two or more times in a row.

What is the minimum number of commands required to reach (x, y) from (0, 0)?

Input

The first line contains a single integer t (1 ≤ t ≤ 100) — the number of testcases.

Each of the next t lines contains two integers x and y (0 ≤ x, y ≤ 10^4) — the destination coordinates of the robot.

Output

For each testcase print a single integer — the minimum number of commands required for the robot to reach (x, y) from (0, 0) if no command is allowed to be executed two or more times in a row.

Example

Input


5
5 5
3 4
7 1
0 0
2 0


Output


10
7
13
0
3

Note

The explanations for the example test:

We use characters N, E, S, W and 0 to denote going north, going east, going south, going west and staying in the current cell, respectively.

In the first test case, the robot can use the following sequence: NENENENENE.

In the second test case, the robot can use the following sequence: NENENEN.

In the third test case, the robot can use the following sequence: ESENENE0ENESE.

In the fourth test case, the robot doesn't need to go anywhere at all.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    result = max(x + y, 2 * max(x, y) - 1)
    print(result)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There is an infinite 2-dimensional grid. The robot stands in cell (0, 0) and wants to reach cell (x, y). Here is a list of possible commands the robot can execute:

  * move north from cell (i, j) to (i, j + 1); 
  * move east from cell (i, j) to (i + 1, j); 
  * move south from cell (i, j) to (i, j - 1); 
  * move west from cell (i, j) to (i - 1, j); 
  * stay in cell (i, j). 



The robot wants to reach cell (x, y) in as few commands as possible. However, he can't execute the same command two or more times in a row.

What is the minimum number of commands required to reach (x, y) from (0, 0)?

Input

The first line contains a single integer t (1 ≤ t ≤ 100) — the number of testcases.

Each of the next t lines contains two integers x and y (0 ≤ x, y ≤ 10^4) — the destination coordinates of the robot.

Output

For each testcase print a single integer — the minimum number of commands required for the robot to reach (x, y) from (0, 0) if no command is allowed to be executed two or more times in a row.

Example

Input


5
5 5
3 4
7 1
0 0
2 0


Output


10
7
13
0
3

The explanations for the example test:

We use characters N, E, S, W and 0 to denote going north, going east, going south, going west and staying in the current cell, respectively.

In the first test case, the robot can use the following sequence: NENENENENE.

In the second test case, the robot can use the following sequence: NENENEN.

In the third test case, the robot can use the following sequence: ESENENE0ENESE.

In the fourth test case, the robot doesn't need to go anywhere at all.

In the fifth test case, the robot can use the following sequence: E0E.

## Problem Understanding

1. Robot starts at (0,0) and wants to reach (x,y)
2. Can move in 4 directions (N, E, S, W) or stay (0)
3. Cannot execute the same command twice in a row
4. Need to find minimum number of commands

## Key Insights

1. **Base case**: If x=0 and y=0, answer is 0 (already at destination)

2. **Basic movements**: To move x units east and y units north, we need at least x + y moves (if no restrictions).

3. **Constraint**: Can't repeat the same command consecutively. This means we can't do "EE" or "NN" directly.

4. **Optimization strategy**: 
   - If we have both x > 0 and y > 0, we can alternate between east and north moves (E,N,E,N,...) which covers both directions efficiently.
   - The minimum moves would be x + y, but we need to check if this is possible due to the no-consecutive-repetition constraint.

If x + y moves is possible, we can arrange them as alternating moves. For example:
- If x ≥ 1 and y ≥ 1: we can do E,N,E,N,... which alternates and satisfies the constraint
- If only x > 0 and y = 0: we cannot do EE, so we need to insert "0" (stay) between consecutive east moves
- If only y > 0 and x = 0: we cannot do NN, so we need to insert "0" between consecutive north moves

And we use 1 N move and 5 stays (since 6 breakers total - 1 N = 5 stays).

So total moves = 7 + 6 = 13.

General pattern:
- If both x > 0 and y > 0: we need x moves of one direction, y moves of another. Since we can alternate, the constraint is not binding if x and y are both positive. But if one is much larger, we still need to break the long sequence.

To place 7 E's with only one N, and no two E's adjacent, we need to ensure that between any two E's there's at least one non-E move.

This is equivalent to placing 7 E's with at least one "gap" between each pair of consecutive E's.

For k moves of one direction and m moves of another (where m ≥ 1), we can always arrange them such that no two of the same direction are consecutive, as long as we have at least one move of the other direction.

But in this case, we only have one N move. So we can separate at most 2 E sequences.

Actually, with one N move, we can separate at most 2 consecutive E blocks. We need 7 E moves.

To place 7 E moves with no two consecutive, we need at least 6 non-E moves to separate them. But we only have 1 N move.

For k east moves and m north moves (k > 0, m > 0), the minimum number of moves is: k + m + max(0, k - 1) ?

Alternative approach: 
- The minimum number of moves is always at least |x| + |y| (Manhattan distance)
- Due to the constraint, if we have only one type of move (say only east), we need 2x - 1 moves 
- If we have both types, we might be able to achieve x + y moves, but only if we can arrange them without consecutive duplicates.

For both x > 0 and y > 0, we can achieve exactly x + y moves by alternating. For example, E,N,E,N,... 

But if one is much larger than the other, we still can alternate. For (7,1), if we do E,N,E,N,E,N,E,N,E,N,E,N,E → that's 13 moves (7 E's and 6 N's), but we only need 1 N move.

We don't need exactly 1 N move, we just need at least 1 N move.

In fact, for reaching (7,1), we can do: E,N,E,E → can't do EE.

We must have at least 6 stays or N moves to separate the 7 E's.

The minimum number of non-E moves needed to separate 7 E's is 6 (to create 7 gaps).

We have 1 N move, so we need 5 more stays to make up the 6 gaps.

So total moves = 7 (E) + 6 (non-E) = 13.

In general, for (x,y):
- We need x east moves and y north moves total
- To avoid consecutive E's, we need at least (x - 1) non-E moves if x > 0
- To avoid consecutive N's, we need at least (y - 1) non-N moves if y > 0
- But non-E moves include both N and stay moves
- Similarly for non-N moves

Actually, the total number of moves = x + y + (extra breaks needed)

The constraint is violated only if we have more than one consecutive same move.

- If x = 0 and y = 0: 0
- If x > 0 and y = 0: 2x - 1  
- If x = 0 and y > 0: 2y - 1
- If x > 0 and y > 0: x + y + max(0, x - 1) + max(0, y - 1) - max(0, x-1, y-1)? 

Wait, this is getting complicated.

Let me think: we need to achieve x east and y north. The total moves is at least x + y.

The constraint is that we can't have two consecutive E's or two consecutive N's.

If we have x > 0 and y > 0, we can alternate between E and N, which means no two consecutive same moves. But we need exactly x E's and y N's.

Can we arrange x E's and y N's such that no two are consecutive?

This is possible if and only if x + y ≥ max(x, y) and we can interleave them properly.

Actually, we can always interleave x E's and y N's to avoid consecutive same moves if at least one of x or y is greater than 0, by placing them alternately.

For example, if x ≥ 1 and y ≥ 1, we can do E,N,E,N,... which works as long as the counts allow.

The only issue is when we have many E's and only one N, we can't place them without having two E's together.

For x E's and y N's, to avoid consecutive E's, we need at least (x - 1) non-E moves.

Similarly, to avoid consecutive N's, we need at least (y - 1) non-N moves.

Total non-consecutive moves needed = (x - 1) + (y - 1) = x + y - 2

But these non-consecutive moves are already included in the total moves.

Actually, the total moves is x + y + additional moves if we can't avoid the consecutive constraints.

Wait, no. The total moves is just the sum of all moves.

If we have x E's and y N's, and we arrange them in a sequence where no two E's are consecutive and no two N's are consecutive, then the total moves is x + y.

This is possible if and only if we can interleave them without consecutive duplicates.

The condition for being able to arrange x E's and y N's with no two consecutive E's and no two consecutive N's is that max(x, y) ≤ min(x + y - 1, 1)? 

Actually, think about it: if we have many E's and few N's, we need to break up the E's.

The maximum number of E's we can place without consecutive duplicates when we have y N's is y + 1 (like E,N,E,N,...,E).

So to place x E's without consecutive duplicates, we need x ≤ y + 1.

Similarly, to place y N's without consecutive duplicates, we need y ≤ x + 1.

Therefore:
- If x > y + 1 or y > x + 1, then we cannot achieve x + y moves without having consecutive duplicates.
- In this case, we need to add stay moves to break sequences.

For example, if x > y + 1, then even with y N's, we can place at most y + 1 E's without consecutive duplicates. So we need additional E's, which means we need to add stay moves.

The number of extra stays needed: if x > y + 1, then we need (x - (y + 1)) more E's, which means we need (x - (y + 1)) more stay moves to separate them.

Similarly if y > x + 1, we need (y - (x + 1)) more stay moves.

But we can also use the stay moves to break sequences.

So total moves = x + y + max(0, x - (y + 1)) + max(0, y - (x + 1))

Wait, let me check with examples:

(5,5): x=5,y=5
max(0, 5-(5+1)) + max(0, 5-(5+1)) = max(0, -1) + max(0, -1) = 0
total = 5+5+0 = 10 ✓

(3,4): x=3,y=4
max(0, 3-(4+1)) + max(0, 4-(3+1)) = max(0, -2) + max(0, 0) = 0
total = 3+4+0 = 7 ✓

(7,1): x=7,y=1

total = 7+1+5 = 13 ✓

(2,0): x=2,y=0
max(0,2-(0+1)) + max(0,0-(2+1)) = max(0,1) + max(0,-3) = 1 + 0 = 1
total = 2+0+1 = 3 ✓

(0,0): 0 ✓

Perfect! This formula works.

But wait, what about the case where y=0 (only east)?

According to our formula:
max(0, x - (0+1)) + max(0, 0 - (x+1)) = max(0, x-1) + 0 = x-1
total = x + 0 + (x-1) = 2x - 1 ✓

Similarly for x=0, y>0: total = 2y - 1 ✓

And for both positive: the formula works as shown.

So the general solution is:
- If x == 0 and y == 0: 0
- Otherwise: x + y + max(0, x - (y + 1)) + max(0, y - (x + 1))

But notice that max(0, x - (y+1)) + max(0, y - (x+1)) = max(0, x - y - 1, y - x - 1)

Actually, this is the same as: max(0, |x - y| - 1)

Because:
- If x ≥ y+1, then x - y - 1 ≥ 0, and y - x - 1 ≤ -1
- If y ≥ x+1, then y - x - 1 ≥ 0, and x - y - 1 ≤ -1
- If |x-y| ≤ 1, then |x-y| - 1 ≤ 0, so max(0, |x-y|-1) = 0

So we can simplify to: x + y + max(0, |x - y| - 1)

Let me verify:
(5,5): |5-5|-1 = 0, total = 10 ✓
(3,4): |3-4|-1 = 0, total = 7 ✓  
(7,1): |7-1|-1 = 5, total = 8+5 = 13 ✓
(2,0): |2-0|-1 = 1, total = 2+1 = 3 ✓
(0,0): 0 ✓

Perfect!

## Final Solution

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    if x == 0 and y == 0:
        print(0)
    else:
        print(x + y + max(0, abs(x - y) - 1))
```

But the expression is correct.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    print(x + y + max(0, abs(x - y) - 1))
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There is an infinite 2-dimensional grid. The robot stands in cell (0, 0) and wants to reach cell (x, y). Here is a list of possible commands the robot can execute:

  * move north from cell (i, j) to (i, j + 1); 
  * move east from cell (i, j) to (i + 1, j); 
  * move south from cell (i, j) to (i, j - 1); 
  * move west from cell (i, j) to (i - 1, j); 
  * stay in cell (i, j). 



The robot wants to reach cell (x, y) in as few commands as possible. However, he can't execute the same command two or more times in a row.

What is the minimum number of commands required to reach (x, y) from (0, 0)?

Input

The first line contains a single integer t (1 ≤ t ≤ 100) — the number of testcases.

Each of the next t lines contains two integers x and y (0 ≤ x, y ≤ 10^4) — the destination coordinates of the robot.

Output

For each testcase print a single integer — the minimum number of commands required for the robot to reach (x, y) from (0, 0) if no command is allowed to be executed two or more times in a row.

Example

Input


5
5 5
3 4
7 1
0 0
2 0


Output


10
7
13
0
3

Note

The explanations for the example test:

We use characters N, E, S, W and 0 to denote going north, going east, going south, going west and staying in the current cell, respectively.

In the first test case, the robot can use the following sequence: NENENENENE.

In the second test case, the robot can use the following sequence: NENENEN.

In the third test case, the robot can use the following sequence: ESENENE0ENESE.

In the fourth test case, the robot doesn't need to go anywhere at all.

In the fifth test case, the robot can use the following sequence: E0E.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

We also need at least 6 non-E commands to separate the 7 E's (since 7 E's need 6 separators between them).

So 2b + 1 ≥ 6 → 2b ≥ 5 → b ≥ 3 (since b integer) → b ≥ 3 → b=3, then a=4, non-E=7.

Total commands = E + W + N + S + 0 = 7 + 0 + 4 + 3 + 0 = 14.

But we can reduce this? 

Actually, we don't need to have W=0, and we can have E and W.

We need E - W = 7, so E = W + 7.

Let E = w + 7, W = w for some w ≥ 0.

Total E moves: w + 7
Total W moves: w

Non-E commands include N, S, and possibly stay commands.

Total non-E commands = N + S + 0.

N - S = 1.

To avoid consecutive same commands, we need to separate the E moves.

The E moves are at different positions, so we need to ensure that no two E's are consecutive.

This means between any two E's, there must be at least one non-E command.

With (w+7) E's, we need at least (w+6) non-E commands to separate them (since (k+1) E's need k separators).

So total commands = E + W + N + S + 0 = (w+7) + w + N + S + 0 = 2w + 7 + (N + S)

But N + S = (N - S) + 2S = 1 + 2S

So total = 2w + 7 + 1 + 2S = 2w + 2S + 8

And we need: N + S ≥ w + 6 (to separate w+7 E's)

So 1 + 2S ≥ w + 6 → 2S ≥ w + 5 → S ≥ (w+5)/2

Minimum S is ceil((w+5)/2)

So total = 2w + 2*ceil((w+5)/2) + 8

We want to minimize this over w ≥ 0.

Try w = 0:
- S ≥ (0+5)/2 = 2.5 → S ≥ 3
- Total = 0 + 2*3 + 8 = 14

Try w = 1:
- S ≥ (1+5)/2 = 3 → S ≥ 3
- Total = 2*1 + 2*3 + 8 = 2 + 6 + 8 = 16

w=0 gives minimum 14? But example says 13.

We can use stay commands to break up the sequence without adding net movement.

For a given sequence of E's, we need to separate them with non-E commands (which could be N, S, or 0).

The number of non-E commands needed is at least (number of E's - 1) to separate consecutive E's.

We have E moves = w + 7, so we need at least (w + 7 - 1) = w + 6 non-E commands.

We can use these to provide net movement: N - S = 1.

Let T = total non-E commands = N + S + stay_count

We need T ≥ w + 6

And N - S = 1

So N = S + 1

T = (S+1) + S + stay_count = 2S + 1 + stay_count

So 2S + 1 + stay_count ≥ w + 6

Total commands = E + W + N + S + stay_count = (w+7) + w + (S+1) + S + stay_count = 2w + 8 + 2S + stay_count

We want to minimize this subject to 2S + 1 + stay_count ≥ w + 6

Let D = 2S + stay_count, then D ≥ w + 5

Total commands = 2w + 8 + D

We want to minimize 2w + 8 + D where D ≥ w + 5

So minimize 2w + 8 + D = 2w + 8 + (w + 5) + (D - (w + 5)) = 3w + 13 + (D - w - 5)

Since D ≥ w + 5, minimum when D = w + 5

So total commands = 2w + 8 + (w + 5) = 3w + 13

We want to minimize this over w ≥ 0.

Minimum at w = 0: 3*0 + 13 = 13

Yes! So minimum is 13 when w = 0.

For w = 0: 
- E = 7, W = 0
- N - S = 1
- D = 2S + stay_count ≥ 0 + 5 = 5
- Minimum when D = 5
- Total commands = 0 + 8 + 5 = 13

We can achieve this with, for example, S = 2, N = 3, stay_count = 1
- N - S = 1 ✓
- Total non-E commands = 3+2+1 = 6 ≥ 6 needed to separate 7 E's ✓
- Total commands = 7 + 0 + 3 + 2 + 1 = 13

We can arrange the sequence to avoid consecutive commands, for example: E, N, E, S, E, N, E, 0, E, N, E, S, E

But net y = 3 - 2 = 1 ✓.

So for general (x,y) with x>0, y>0:

We need to minimize total commands.

Let me generalize.

We have to have:
- E - W = x
- N - S = y
- Total commands = E + W + N + S + 0

With the constraint that we need at least (E - 1) non-E commands to separate E's (to avoid consecutive E's), similarly at least (N - 1) non-E commands for N's, and similarly for W and S.

Actually, to avoid consecutive same commands, for any direction, if we have k moves in that direction, we need at least (k-1) non-identical commands between them.

But the non-identical commands are shared among all directions.

The total number of non-E commands must be at least max(0, E-1) to avoid consecutive E's.

Similarly, non-N commands must be at least max(0, N-1) to avoid consecutive N's.

But "non-E commands" are the commands that are not E, which include N, S, W, 0.

To avoid consecutive E's, we need at least (E-1) commands that are not E.

Similarly, to avoid consecutive N's, we need at least (N-1) commands that are not N.

To avoid consecutive W's, we need at least (W-1) commands that are not W.

To avoid consecutive S's, we need at least (S-1) commands that are not S.

Let T = total commands = E + W + N + S + 0
Let U = total non-E commands = N + S + W + 0
Let V = total non-N commands = E + S + W + 0

We need:
- U ≥ E - 1  (to avoid consecutive E's)
- V ≥ N - 1  (to avoid consecutive N's)

Also, E - W = x, N - S = y

We want to minimize T = E + W + N + S + 0 = (E - W) + (W + W) + (N - S) + (S + S) + 0 = x + 2W + y + 2S + 0

So T = x + y + 2W + 2S + 0

From E - W = x, E = x + W
From N - S = y, N = y + S

Now, U = N + S + W + 0 = (y + S) + S + W + 0 = y + 2S + W + 0
We need U ≥ E - 1 = (x + W) - 1 = x + W - 1
So y + 2S + W + 0 ≥ x + W - 1
Simplify: y + 2S ≥ x - 1
So 2S ≥ x - y - 1
S ≥ ceil((x - y - 1)/2) if x - y - 1 > 0, else 0

Similarly, V = E + S + W + 0 = (x + W) + S + W + 0 = x + S + 2W + 0
We need V ≥ N - 1 = (y + S) - 1 = y + S - 1
So x + S + 2W ≥ y + S - 1
Simplify: x + 2W ≥ y - 1
So 2W ≥ y - x - 1
W ≥ ceil((y - x - 1)/2) if y - x - 1 > 0, else 0

We have T = x + y + 2W + 2S + 0

We want to minimize T by choosing W ≥ max(0, ceil((y - x - 1)/2)) and S ≥ max(0, ceil((x - y - 1)/2))

Since the constraints are linear and we want to minimize, we should take the minimum possible W and S.

So:
- W_min = max(0, ceil((y - x - 1)/2))
- S_min = max(0, ceil((x - y - 1)/2))

Then T_min = x + y + 2*W_min + 2*S_min + 0

Let me test:
(5,5): 5+5 + max(0,0-1) = 10 + 0 = 10 ✓

(7,1): 7+1 + max(0,7-1-1) = 8 + max(0,5) = 8+5 = 13 ✓

For (1,0): 2*1 - 1 = 1

Total commands: 4 + (at least 3) = 7.

- Net: x=4, y=1-1=0, not good.

- Positions: 0,1,2,3,4,5,6
- E at 0,2,4,6 → 4 times

- Stays at 3,5 → net y=1

- Step0: E, step1: N ≠ E, step2: E ≠ N, step3: 0 ≠ E, step4: E ≠ 0, step5: 0 ≠ E, step6: E
- All good.

Yes! So it works with 7 commands.

So the formula seems to be:
- if x == 0 and y == 0: 0
- if x == 0 and y > 0: 2y - 1
- if x > 0 and y == 0: 2x - 1
- if x > 0 and y > 0: x + y + max(0, abs(x - y) - 1)

But abs(x-y) - 1 might be negative, so max(0, abs(x-y) - 1)

In (3,4): abs(3-4)=1, 1-1=0, so x+y+0=7 ✓
In (7,1): abs(7-1)=6, 6-1=5, 7+1+5=13 ✓
In (5,5): abs(0)=0, 0-1=-1, max(0,-1)=0, 5+5+0=10 ✓

For (1,1): abs(0)=0, 0-1 = -1, max(0,-1)=0, 1+1+0=2 ✓

For (4,1): abs(3)=3, 3-1=2, 4+1+2=7 ✓

Now, is there a unified formula?

Notice that:
- When one coordinate is 0: it's 2*max(x,y) - 1
- When both >0: x+y + max(0, |x-y| - 1)

But 2*max(x,y) - 1 when one is 0.

We can write a unified formula.

Notice that when x>0 and y>0, we have x+y + max(0, |x-y| - 1) = max(x,y) + min(x,y) + max(0, |x-y| - 1)

= max(x,y) + min(x,y) + max(0, |x-y| - 1)

If x>=y: = x + y + max(0, x-y-1) = x + y + max(0, x-y-1)

If x>=y: when x=y, x+y+0 = 2x
When x>y, x+y + (x-y-1) = 2x -1

Similarly, if y>x: 2y-1

So in both cases, when both >0, we get max(2x, 2y) - 1 if x!=y, and 2x if x=y.

When x=y: 2x
When |x-y|>0: 2*max(x,y) - 1

When one is 0: 2*max(x,y) - 1

When both 0: 0

So in all cases (x,y) >= (0,0), the minimum number of commands is:
- 0 if x=0 and y=0
- 2*max(x,y) - 1 if at least one of x,y is 0
- 2*max(x,y) - 1 if x>0 and y>0 and x!=y
- 2*max(x,y) if x=y and x>0

But 2*max(x,y) when x=y is the same as 2*x, and when x!=y, it's 2*max(x,y) - 1.

So in summary:
- if x == 0 and y == 0: 0
- else: 2 * max(x, y) - 1 if x != y
- else (x == y): 2 * x

But when x==y and both>0, 2*x = 2*max(x,y)

We can write: 
result = 0 if x==0 and y==0
result = 2 * max(x, y) - 1 if min(x,y) == 0 or (x != y)
result = 2 * x if x == y and x > 0

But when x==y and x>0, 2*x = 2*max(x,y), which is not 2*max-1.

Notice that when x==y, we have x+y + max(0, |x-y|-1) = x+x + 0 = 2x
When x!=y, we have x+y + (|x-y|-1) = 2*max(x,y) - 1

So actually, the formula is:
result = x + y + max(0, abs(x-y) - 1) if x>0 and y>0
But for one zero, we have a different formula.

For (2,0): x>0, y=0, we need 3 = 2*2 - 1 = 4-1

With the formula x+y+max(0,|x-y|-1) = 2+0 + max(0,2-0-1) = 2 + max(0,1) = 3 ✓

For (3,0): 3+0 + max(0,3-0-1) = 3+2 = 5, and 2*3-1=5 ✓

For (3,1): 3+1 + max(0,|3-1|-1) = 4 + max(0,2-1) = 4+1 = 5
Is 5 achievable? net (3,1)
With 3 E's and 1 N: need to separate E's with non-E commands.
3 E's need at least 2 non-E commands.
We have 1 N, so need at least 1 more non-E command (S or 0).
Total non-E commands: at least 3.
Total commands: 3 + 3 = 6, not 5.

Mistake.

For (3,1): x=3,y=1
We need E - W = 3, N - S = 1
Try to minimize.

We can do: E, N, E, 0, E
- Net: x=3, y=1 ✓
- Commands: E,N,E,0,E → 5 commands
- No consecutive same: E,N,E,0,E → all different from previous ✓

Yes! So 5 commands.

With the formula: x+y+max(0,|x-y|-1) = 3+1 + max(0,|3-1|-1) = 4 + max(0,2-1) = 4+1 = 5 ✓

For (3,0): x=3,y=0
- Formula: 3+0 + max(0,|3-0|-1) = 3 + max(0,3-1) = 3+2 = 5
- But earlier we said for (2,0) should be 3, and for (3,0) should be 5? 2*3-1=5 ✓

Yes.

For (0,0): 0+0 + max(0,0-0-1) = 0 + max(0,-1) = 0 ✓

So the unified formula is:
result = x + y + max(0, abs(x - y) - 1)

Let me verify all examples:
- (5,5): 5+5 + max(0,0-1) = 10 + 0 = 10 ✓
- (3,4): 3+4 + max(0,1-1) = 7 + 0 = 7 ✓
- (7,1): 7+1 + max(0,6-1) = 8 + 5 = 13 ✓
- (0,0): 0+0 + max(0,0-0-1) = 0 ✓
- (2,0): 2+0 + max(0,2-0-1) = 2 + max(0,1) = 2+1 = 3 ✓

Perfect!

The formula works for all cases.

## Final Solution

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    result = x + y + max(0, abs(x - y) - 1)
    print(result)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There is an infinite 2-dimensional grid. The robot stands in cell (0, 0) and wants to reach cell (x, y). Here is a list of possible commands the robot can execute:

  * move north from cell (i, j) to (i, j + 1); 
  * move east from cell (i, j) to (i + 1, j); 
  * move south from cell (i, j) to (i, j - 1); 
  * move west from cell (i, j) to (i - 1, j); 
  * stay in cell (i, j). 



The robot wants to reach cell (x, y) in as few commands as possible. However, he can't execute the same command two or more times in a row.

What is the minimum number of commands required to reach (x, y) from (0, 0)?

Input

The first line contains a single integer t (1 ≤ t ≤ 100) — the number of testcases.

Each of the next t lines contains two integers x and y (0 ≤ x, y ≤ 10^4) — the destination coordinates of the robot.

Output

For each testcase print a single integer — the minimum number of commands required for the robot to reach (x, y) from (0, 0) if no command is allowed to be executed two or more times in a row.

Example

Input


5

3 4
7 1
0 0
2 0


Output


10

13
0
3

We use characters N, E, S, W and 0 to denote going north, going east, going south, going west and staying in the current cell, respectively.

In the first test case, the robot can use the following sequence: NENENENENE.

In the second test case, the robot can use the following sequence: NENENEN.

In the third test case, the robot can use the following sequence: ESENENE0ENESE.

In the fourth test case, the robot doesn't need to go anywhere at all.

In the fifth test case, the robot can use the following sequence: E0E.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a robot starting at (0,0) that wants to reach (x,y)
2. The robot can move in 4 directions (N, E, S, W) or stay (0)
3. **Constraint**: No command can be executed twice in a row
4. We need to find the minimum number of commands

## Key Insights

1. **Basic movement**: To reach (x,y), we need at least |x| + |y| moves (x east/west + y north/south)
2. **Constraint issue**: We can't repeat the same command consecutively
3. **Staying option**: We can use '0' (stay) to break repeated command sequences

## Analysis of Constraints

The main challenge is the "no consecutive same commands" rule. Let's think about when we need to use the stay command:

- If we need to move in only one direction (say only east), we cannot just do "EEEE" - that would have 4 consecutive E's
- We need to insert "stay" or alternate directions to break the pattern

## Case Analysis

Let's consider different scenarios:

**Case 1: (0,0)** - Already at destination: answer is 0

**Case 2: Only one coordinate non-zero** (like (x,0) or (0,y))
- We need |x| or |y| moves
- But we can't have consecutive same moves
- We need to alternate with stay commands
- For example, to go (2,0): E0E (3 commands)
- Pattern: Move, Stay, Move, Stay... (for odd distances) or Move, Move (for even distances? Wait...)

Actually, for (2,0):
- E0E = 3 commands
- E,E has two consecutive E's - not allowed
- So we need at least 3 commands for distance 2

**Case 3: Both coordinates non-zero**
- We need |x| + |y| movements
- But we need to ensure no consecutive same commands
- We can alternate between different directions

## Key Insight: When is the constraint violated?

The constraint is only violated when we have consecutive identical moves. 

The optimal strategy is to alternate between different directions. For example, we can use a pattern like N,E,N,E,... or N,E,S,W,...

But we also have the stay command as an option.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    if x == 0 and y == 0:
        print(0)
    elif x == 0 or y == 0:
        print(2 * max(x, y) - 1)
    else:
        print(x + y + max(0, max(x, y) - min(x, y) - 1))
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There is an infinite 2-dimensional grid. The robot stands in cell (0, 0) and wants to reach cell (x, y). Here is a list of possible commands the robot can execute:

  * move north from cell (i, j) to (i, j + 1); 
  * move east from cell (i, j) to (i + 1, j); 
  * move south from cell (i, j) to (i, j - 1); 
  * move west from cell (i, j) to (i - 1, j); 
  * stay in cell (i, j). 



The robot wants to reach cell (x, y) in as few commands as possible. However, he can't execute the same command two or more times in a row.

What is the minimum number of commands required to reach (x, y) from (0, 0)?

Input

The first line contains a single integer t (1 ≤ t ≤ 100) — the number of testcases.

Each of the next t lines contains two integers x and y (0 ≤ x, y ≤ 10^4) — the destination coordinates of the robot.

Output

For each testcase print a single integer — the minimum number of commands required for the robot to reach (x, y) from (0, 0) if no command is allowed to be executed two or more times in a row.

Example

Input


5
5 5
3 4
7 1
0 0
2 0


Output


10
7
13
0
3

Note

The explanations for the example test:

We use characters N, E, S, W and 0 to denote going north, going east, going south, going west and staying in the current cell, respectively.

In the first test case, the robot can use the following sequence: NENENENENE.

In the second test case, the robot can use the following sequence: NENENEN.

In the third test case, the robot can use the following sequence: ESENENE0ENESE.

In the fourth test case, the robot doesn't need to go anywhere at all.

In the fifth test case, the robot can use the following sequence: E0E.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

- Otherwise: max(x + y, 2 * max(x, y) - 1)

Let me test:
- (5,5): max(10, 2*5-1) = max(10,9) = 10 ✓
- (3,4): max(7, 2*4-1) = max(7,7) = 7 ✓
- (7,1): max(8, 2*7-1) = max(8,13) = 13 ✓
- (2,0): max(2, 2*2-1) = max(2,3) = 3 ✓
- (0,0): 0 ✓

This matches all examples!

Why does this work?

- When both x and y are positive, we can use a sequence that alternates between the two directions (e.g., E,N,E,N,...) to reach (x,y) in exactly x+y moves.
- When one of them is zero, say x > 0, y = 0, then we need x east moves and can't have consecutive E's. We must insert at least (x-1) stay commands to separate the x E's (like E,0,E,0,...,E). This takes 2x-1 commands.
- The max(x+y, 2*max(x,y)-1) ensures that we take the larger of these two values.

For example, when both are positive, x+y might be smaller than 2*max(x,y)-1, but in practice for both positive, x+y is at least as large as 2*max(x,y)-1 only in specific cases.

For example, (3,4): x+y=7, 2*max-1=7, equal.
For (5,5): 10 vs 9, so x+y wins.
For (1,1): 2 vs 1, so x+y wins.

In fact, when both are positive, is x+y >= 2*max(x,y)-1?

x+y >= 2*max(x,y)-1
=> x+y >= 2*max - 1
=> x+y >= 2*max - 1

Since max = max(x,y), let's say max=x, then y <= x
So x+y >= 2x - 1
=> y >= x - 1

This is true when y >= x-1, which is true for most values, but not always.

For example, if x=3, y=1: x+y=4, 2*3-1=5, so 4 < 5.

Yes.

So the formula is: max(x + y, 2 * max(x, y) - 1)

In general, for any (x,y), the minimum number of commands is:
- 0 if both x and y are 0
- Otherwise: max(x + y, 2 * max(x, y) - 1)

This works because:
1. When both coordinates are positive, we can always achieve x+y moves by alternating, but only if x+y >= 2*max-1. If not, we need more because of the separation constraints.
2. The max function ensures we take the larger value.

Actually, is x+y always >= 2*max-1 when both are positive?
No, as shown with (3,1): 3+1=4, 2*3-1=5, 4<5.

So we must take max(x+y, 2*max-1).

Therefore, the solution is:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    if x == 0 and y == 0:
        print(0)
    else:
        result = max(x + y, 2 * max(x, y) - 1)
        print(result)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There is an infinite 2-dimensional grid. The robot stands in cell (0, 0) and wants to reach cell (x, y). Here is a list of possible commands the robot can execute:

  * move north from cell (i, j) to (i, j + 1); 
  * move east from cell (i, j) to (i + 1, j); 
  * move south from cell (i, j) to (i, j - 1); 
  * move west from cell (i, j) to (i - 1, j); 
  * stay in cell (i, j). 



The robot wants to reach cell (x, y) in as few commands as possible. However, he can't execute the same command two or more times in a row.

What is the minimum number of commands required to reach (x, y) from (0, 0)?

Input

The first line contains a single integer t (1 ≤ t ≤ 100) — the number of testcases.

Each of the next t lines contains two integers x and y (0 ≤ x, y ≤ 10^4) — the destination coordinates of the robot.

Output

For each testcase print a single integer — the minimum number of commands required for the robot to reach (x, y) from (0, 0) if no command is allowed to be executed two or more times in a row.

Example

Input


5
5 5
3 4
7 1
0 0
2 0


Output


10
7
13
0
3

Note

The explanations for the example test:

We use characters N, E, S, W and 0 to denote going north, going east, going south, going west and staying in the current cell, respectively.

In the first test case, the robot can use the following sequence: NENENENENE.

In the second test case, the robot can use the following sequence: NENENEN.

In the third test case, the robot can use the following sequence: ESENENE0ENESE.

In the fourth test case, the robot doesn't need to go anywhere at all.

In the fifth test case, the robot can use the following sequence: E0E.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

In general:
- If x = 0 or y = 0: answer = 2*max(x,y) - 1
- If x > 0 and y > 0: we need x + y moves, but only if we can arrange them without consecutive duplicates

When can we arrange x east and y north moves in x+y moves without consecutive duplicates?
- We can do it if we can alternate between E and N.
- This is possible if at least one of x or y is at least 1 (which it is), and we have both.
- Actually, as long as min(x,y) > 0, we can interleave them.

In the pattern ENE...NE, if we have x E's and y N's, we need to ensure that no two adjacent moves are the same.

For any alternating sequence of E and N, the counts can differ by at most 1.

For example:
- Pattern starting with E: E N E N ... → if length is even: count_E = count_N
- If length is odd: count_E = count_N + 1
- Pattern starting with N: N E N E ... → if length is even: count_E = count_N

I think I misunderstood the problem. We can use stay commands, but we're trying to minimize total commands.

For (7,1): answer is 13, which is 7 + 1 + 5 = 13.
5 stay commands.

The pattern is: to avoid consecutive E's, we need at least (x-1) non-E moves when x > 1.
Similarly, to avoid consecutive N's, we need at least (y-1) non-N moves when y > 1.

But non-E moves can be N or stay, and non-N moves can be E or stay.

The total number of non-E moves needed is max(0, x-1) to separate E's.
The total number of non-N moves needed is max(0, y-1) to separate N's.

But these are not necessarily disjoint - stay commands can serve both purposes.

Actually, a stay command serves as both a non-E and a non-N move.

So we need:
- At least max(0, x-1) non-E moves to separate the E's
- At least max(0, y-1) non-N moves to separate the N's

The total number of additional commands (beyond the x+y moves) is at least max(max(0, x-1), max(0, y-1))?

No, because the non-E moves include N and stay, and non-N moves include E and stay.

The total number of moves is: (x + y) + (additional moves)

The additional moves are needed to satisfy the separation constraints.

We need at least (x-1) non-E moves to separate E's (if x > 1).
We need at least (y-1) non-N moves to separate N's (if y > 1).

The non-E moves are either N or stay.
The non-N moves are either E or stay.

But we can't create new E's or N's.

The total number of non-E moves required is max(0, x-1) (to separate x E's).
The total number of non-N moves required is max(0, y-1) (to separate y N's).

These constraints must be satisfied by the existing N moves and stay commands.

If we have only y N's, they can serve as non-E moves (since N ≠ E), and stay commands can serve as both.

So:
- The N moves themselves can be used as non-E moves
- The stay commands can be used as non-E moves and non-N moves

Therefore:
- We need at least max(0, x-1) non-E moves in total
- We need at least max(0, y-1) non-N moves in total

The number of non-E moves available = y + s (where s is number of stay commands)
The number of non-N moves available = x + s (where s is number of stay commands)

We need:
1. y + s ≥ max(0, x-1)
2. x + s ≥ max(0, y-1)

We want to minimize total commands = x + y + s

If x = 0 and y = 0: s = 0
If x = 0 and y > 0: we need y north moves, and no E moves. But cannot have NN consecutively, so we need at least (y-1) non-N moves. Non-N moves come from stay commands. So s ≥ (y-1). Total = 0 + y + (y-1) = 2y - 1
If y = 0 and x > 0: similarly, s ≥ (x-1), total = x + 0 + (x-1) = 2x - 1
If x > 0 and y > 0: we need s such that:
- y + s ≥ max(0, x-1)
- x + s ≥ max(0, y-1)

We want to minimize s.

Actually, we can solve this directly.

The minimum s is max(0, x-1 - y, y-1 - x) ? No.

We need s ≥ max(0, x-1 - y, y-1 - x) ?

From y + s ≥ max(0, x-1) → s ≥ max(0, x-1 - y)
From x + s ≥ max(0, y-1) → s ≥ max(0, y-1 - x)

So s ≥ max(0, x-1 - y, y-1 - x)

But x-1 - y and y-1 - x are negatives of each other, so max(0, x-1 - y, y-1 - x) = max(0, |x-1 - y|, |y-1 - x|) but they are symmetric.

Actually, max(0, x-1 - y, y-1 - x) = max(0, x-1-y, y-1-x)

But x-1-y = (x-y)-1, y-1-x = (y-x)-1

So max(0, (x-y)-1, (y-x)-1) = max(0, |x-y|-1)

Because if x > y, then (x-y)-1, if x < y, then (y-x)-1, and if x=y, both are -1.

So s ≥ max(0, |x-y| - 1)

Therefore, total commands = x + y + max(0, |x-y| - 1)

Let me verify with examples:

1. (5,5): |5-5|-1 = 0, total = 5+5+0 = 10 ✓
2. (3,4): |3-4|-1 = 1-1 = 0, total = 3+4+0 = 7 ✓  
3. (7,1): |7-1|-1 = 6-1 = 5, total = 7+1+5 = 13 ✓
4. (0,0): 0+0+0 = 0 ✓
5. (2,0): |2-0|-1 = 2-1 = 1, total = 2+0+1 = 3 ✓

Perfect! All match the expected outputs.

## Final Solution

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    if x == 0 and y == 0:
        print(0)
    else:
        result = x + y + max(0, abs(x - y) - 1)
        print(result)
```

Wait, for (0,0) it works as well since abs(0-0)-1 = -1, max(0,-1)=0, so 0+0+0=0.

We can simplify:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    print(x + y + max(0, abs(x - y) - 1))
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There is an infinite 2-dimensional grid. The robot stands in cell (0, 0) and wants to reach cell (x, y). Here is a list of possible commands the robot can execute:

  * move north from cell (i, j) to (i, j + 1); 
  * move east from cell (i, j) to (i + 1, j); 
  * move south from cell (i, j) to (i, j - 1); 
  * move west from cell (i, j) to (i - 1, j); 
  * stay in cell (i, j). 



The robot wants to reach cell (x, y) in as few commands as possible. However, he can't execute the same command two or more times in a row.

What is the minimum number of commands required to reach (x, y) from (0, 0)?

Input

The first line contains a single integer t (1 ≤ t ≤ 100) — the number of testcases.

Each of the next t lines contains two integers x and y (0 ≤ x, y ≤ 10^4) — the destination coordinates of the robot.

Output

For each testcase print a single integer — the minimum number of commands required for the robot to reach (x, y) from (0, 0) if no command is allowed to be executed two or more times in a row.

Example

Input


5
5 5
3 4
7 1
0 0
2 0


Output


10
7
13
0
3

Note

The explanations for the example test:

We use characters N, E, S, W and 0 to denote going north, going east, going south, going west and staying in the current cell, respectively.

In the first test case, the robot can use the following sequence: NENENENENE.

In the second test case, the robot can use the following sequence: NENENEN.

In the third test case, the robot can use the following sequence: ESENENE0ENESE.

In the fourth test case, the robot doesn't need to go anywhere at all.

In the fifth test case, the robot can use the following sequence: E0E.

## Key Insights

2. **Constraint impact**: We can't repeat the same command twice in a row. This means if we have to make many moves in the same direction, we need to intersperse other moves (like staying or moving in a different direction).

3. **When is the constraint meaningful?**
   - If we need to make many moves in one direction, we have to break up consecutive moves
   - For example, if we need to move 5 steps east, we can't just do "EEEEEE" - we need to insert other commands between them
   - We can insert "stay" (0) or moves in other directions

   - The constraint is only problematic when we need to make large numbers of moves in the same direction
   - The minimum number of commands = base moves + extra moves needed to break up consecutive identical commands

   - When both are non-zero: we can alternate between directions

We also have the constraint that no two consecutive identical commands.

If w > 0, we have extra W moves and E moves.

For example, if w=0: total = 8 moves. Can we arrange 7 E's and 1 N with no consecutive identical commands?

We need to place 1 N among 7 E's. If we place N in between E's, like E,N,E,E,E,E,E - has consecutive E's.

We need to separate the E's.

The sequence must have no two consecutive E's.

So if we have 7 E's and 1 N, we need at least 6 separators between them, but we have only 1 N.

Actually, to avoid consecutive E's, we need at least one non-E command between every two E's.

With 7 E's, we need at least 6 positions to separate them (between first and second, second and third, ..., sixth and seventh).

We have only 1 N command, so we can only break 1 connection.

If we place N between E's, we break only one connection.

For example: E,N,E,E,E,E,E - still has consecutive E's after the N.

To prevent consecutive E's, we need to interleave them with other commands.

The minimum number of non-E commands needed to separate 7 E's is 6 (one between each pair).

Since we only have one N, we need at least 5 more non-E commands (like stay or S or W).

So minimum additional commands = 6 - 1 = 5.

Therefore total moves = 7 (E) + 1 (N) + 5 (additional) = 13.

Ah! This makes sense.

In general, if we have a sequence of moves in a particular direction that we need to make many times, we need to break them up with other commands.

Specifically, for a sequence of k moves in one direction, to avoid consecutive identical commands, we need at least (k-1) break commands (any command that is different from the current direction).

But if we also have moves in the perpendicular direction, these can serve as breaks.

In this case, for the east direction with 7 moves, we need to break up the 7 E's into 7 separate segments, requiring at least 6 break points.

We have only 1 north move, and it can serve as one break point.

So we need 6 - 1 = 5 additional break points (which can be stay or south or west moves).

Thus total commands = 7 + 1 + 5 = 13.

The general rule:
- To make k moves in one direction, we need at least (k-1) break commands between them
- Each break command can be a different command (N, S, W, or 0)
- We can use the moves in the other direction as break commands (each will serve as one break)
- The number of break commands needed = max(0, k - 1 - m) where m is the number of moves in the perpendicular direction

Wait, not exactly.

Actually, if we have k moves in direction A, and m moves in direction B, then the number of "breaks" we need is max(0, k - 1 - m), because the m moves in direction B can each serve as a break point between adjacent A moves.

But we also have to consider that the break commands are part of the total move count.

So total moves = k + m + max(0, k - 1 - m)

This simplifies to: k + m + max(0, k - 1 - m) = 
- if k <= m+1: k + m
- if k > m+1: k + m + (k - 1 - m) = 2k - 1

Similarly, if we have more in the y-direction, it would be 2y - 1.

But since we have both x and y, we can choose which direction has more moves.

For (7,1): x=7, y=1
max(0, 7-1-1) = max(0,5) = 5
total = 7 + 1 + 5 = 13 ✓

For (5,5): x=5, y=5
max(0, 5-1-5) = max(0,-1) = 0
total = 5 + 5 + 0 = 10 ✓

For (3,4): x=3, y=4
max(0, 3-1-4) = max(0,-2) = 0
total = 3 + 4 + 0 = 7 ✓

For (2,0): x=2, y=0
max(0, 2-1-0) = 1
total = 2 + 0 + 1 = 3 ✓

For (0,0): 0 ✓

The formula is: x + y + max(0, max(x,y) - 1 - min(x,y)) = x + y + max(0, |x - y| - 1)

Since max(x,y) - min(x,y) = |x - y|

So total moves = x + y + max(0, |x - y| - 1)

But for (3,4): |3-4| = 1, max(0,1-1) = 0, so 3+4+0 = 7 ✓
For (7,1): |7-1| = 6, max(0,6-1) = 5, so 7+1+5 = 13 ✓
For (5,5): |5-5| = 0, max(0,0-1) = 0, so 10 ✓
For (2,0): |2-0| = 2, max(0,2-1) = 1, so 2+0+1 = 3 ✓

Perfect!

The general solution is:
- If x == 0 and y == 0: 0
- Otherwise: x + y + max(0, abs(x - y) - 1)

Wait, is this always true?

Let me verify with a small example: (1,0)
x=1, y=0
|1-0| = 1, max(0,1-1) = 0
total = 1+0+0 = 1 ✓

(2,0): 2+0+max(0,2-1)=2+1=3 ✓

(3,0): 3+0+max(0,3-1)=3+2=5

Can we do it in 5 moves?
- We need 3 east moves, no north, so we need to break consecutive E's.
- We can do: E, 0, E, 0, E → 5 moves, no consecutive same commands. ✓
- We can do: E, S, E, 0, E → also works. ✓

So yes, 5 moves is minimum.

Another example: (4,1)
|4-1| = 3, max(0,3-1)=2
total = 4+1+2 = 7

We need 4 E's and 1 N.
We need 3 break points between E's.
1 N can serve as one break point.
Need 2 more break points (can be 0 or S or W).
Total moves: 4+1+2 = 7.

Sequence: E, N, E, 0, E, 0, E → this has 4 E's, 1 N, 2 zeros → total 7 moves, and no two consecutive identical commands (E is separated by N and 0).

Yes.

Therefore, the final formula is:
**answer = x + y + max(0, abs(x - y) - 1)**

Or simplified: **answer = x + y + max(0, abs(x - y) - 1)**

But let's write it as:
- if x == 0 and y == 0: 0
- else: x + y + max(0, abs(x - y) - 1)

Actually, when x=0,y=0: abs(0-0)-1 = -1, max(0,-1)=0, so x+y+0 = 0 ✓

So it works for all cases including (0,0).

Therefore, the solution is simply:

For each test case:
- Read x, y
- Print x + y + max(0, abs(x - y) - 1)

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    result = x + y + max(0, abs(x - y) - 1)
    print(result)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There is an infinite 2-dimensional grid. The robot stands in cell (0, 0) and wants to reach cell (x, y). Here is a list of possible commands the robot can execute:

  * move north from cell (i, j) to (i, j + 1); 
  * move east from cell (i, j) to (i + 1, j); 
  * move south from cell (i, j) to (i, j - 1); 
  * move west from cell (i, j) to (i - 1, j); 
  * stay in cell (i, j). 



The robot wants to reach cell (x, y) in as few commands as possible. However, he can't execute the same command two or more times in a row.

What is the minimum number of commands required to reach (x, y) from (0, 0)?

Input

The first line contains a single integer t (1 ≤ t ≤ 100) — the number of testcases.

Each of the next t lines contains two integers x and y (0 ≤ x, y ≤ 10^4) — the destination coordinates of the robot.

Output

For each testcase print a single integer — the minimum number of commands required for the robot to reach (x, y) from (0, 0) if no command is allowed to be executed two or more times in a row.

Example

Input


5
5 5
3 4
7 1
0 0
2 0


Output


10
7
13
0
3

Note

The explanations for the example test:

We use characters N, E, S, W and 0 to denote going north, going east, going south, going west and staying in the current cell, respectively.

In the first test case, the robot can use the following sequence: NENENENENE.

In the second test case, the robot can use the following sequence: NENENEN.

In the third test case, the robot can use the following sequence: ESENENE0ENESE.

In the fourth test case, the robot doesn't need to go anywhere at all.

In the fifth test case, the robot can use the following sequence: E0E.

But why is this valid?

When |x-y| = 0: we have x=y, so we can alternate perfectly with x+y = 2x commands.

When |x-y| = 1: we can alternate almost perfectly, with one direction having one more move. We can place the extra move at the end, and since it's alternating, no two consecutive same commands.

When |x-y| ≥ 2: we need to separate the larger set into groups. The difference in count is d = |x-y|, so we need (d-1) additional break points (separators) beyond what the smaller count provides. These separations are achieved using stays.

For example, with 7E and 1N: we need 6 separators to break 7 E's into 7 pieces, but we only have 1 N to use as a separator. So we need 5 additional stays, total separators = 6, total commands = 7+1+5 = 13.

The formula is: total_commands = x + y + max(0, |x-y| - 1)

But when |x-y| = 0, max(0, -1) = 0, so x+y+0 = x+y.

But when |x-y| = 0: |x-y|-1 = -1, so max(0, -1) = 0.

When |x-y| = 1: |x-y|-1 = 0, so max(0,0) = 0.

When |x-y| = 2: |x-y|-1 = 1, so max(0,1) = 1.

So the formula is: x + y + max(0, |x-y| - 1)

This works for all cases.

Therefore, the solution is:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    if x == 0 and y == 0:
        print(0)
    else:
        diff = abs(x - y)
        result = x + y + max(0, diff - 1)
        print(result)
```

Wait, but when x=0, y=0: our formula gives 0+0+max(0,0-1)=0, so it works.

```python
t = int(input())
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

We have a tree with N vertices. The vertices are numbered 0 through N - 1, and the i-th edge (0 ≤ i < N - 1) comnnects Vertex a_i and b_i. For each pair of vertices u and v (0 ≤ u, v < N), we define the distance d(u, v) as the number of edges in the path u-v.

It is expected that one of the vertices will be invaded by aliens from outer space. Snuke wants to immediately identify that vertex when the invasion happens. To do so, he has decided to install an antenna on some vertices.

First, he decides the number of antennas, K (1 ≤ K ≤ N). Then, he chooses K different vertices, x_0, x_1, ..., x_{K - 1}, on which he installs Antenna 0, 1, ..., K - 1, respectively. If Vertex v is invaded by aliens, Antenna k (0 ≤ k < K) will output the distance d(x_k, v). Based on these K outputs, Snuke will identify the vertex that is invaded. Thus, in order to identify the invaded vertex no matter which one is invaded, the following condition must hold:

* For each vertex u (0 ≤ u < N), consider the vector (d(x_0, u), ..., d(x_{K - 1}, u)). These N vectors are distinct.



Find the minumum value of K, the number of antennas, when the condition is satisfied.

Constraints

* 2 ≤ N ≤ 10^5
* 0 ≤ a_i, b_i < N
* The given graph is a tree.

Input

Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:


N
a_0 b_0
a_1 b_1
:
a_{N - 2} b_{N - 2}


Output

Print the minumum value of K, the number of antennas, when the condition is satisfied.

Examples

Input

5
0 1
0 2
0 3
3 4


Output

2

## Problem Understanding

We have a tree with N vertices, and we need to find the minimum number of antennas K such that for any vertex u, the vector of distances from u to each antenna position is unique.

In other words, for any two different vertices u and v, the vector (d(x₀,u), d(x₁,u), ..., d(x_{K-1},u)) must be different from (d(x₀,v), d(x₁,v), ..., d(x_{K-1},v)).

## Key Insights

1. If we have only one antenna, then for any two vertices u and v, we only have one distance. In a tree, if two vertices are at the same distance from a single antenna, they can't be distinguished.

2. If we have two antennas, we can potentially distinguish vertices based on their distance pairs.

3. A crucial observation: In a tree, the most "central" vertices are likely to need more antennas because vertices far from the center might have similar distance patterns.

4. Let's think about when we can distinguish vertices with K antennas. We need to ensure that for any two vertices, there's at least one antenna that sees them at different distances.

5. A key insight: For any vertex u, if we can find a set of antenna positions such that the distance vector to u is unique, we're good.

6. Consider the diameter of the tree - the longest path between any two vertices. If we place antennas at the endpoints of the diameter, we might be able to distinguish vertices well.

7. However, the minimum K might be related to the structure of the tree, particularly regarding symmetry.

Example 1: N=5, edges: 0-1, 0-2, 0-3, 3-4
- This is a star with center 0, and leaf 4 connected to 3.
- With K=1: Can't distinguish between vertices at same distance from center (e.g., 1,2,3 have distance 1 from 0, but 4 has distance 2).
- With K=2: We can place antennas at positions that create distinct distance patterns. For example, place at 0 and 4. 
  - Vertex 0: (0,2)
  - Vertex 1: (1,3) 
  - Vertex 2: (2,3)
  - Vertex 3: (3,1)
  - Vertex 4: (4,0)
  - All vectors are distinct. So K=2 works.

Example 2: N=2, edges: 0-1

```python
import sys
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build the graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n-1):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)
    
    # Count degrees
    degrees = [0] * n
    for i in range(n):
        degrees[i] = len(graph[i])
    
    # Check if the tree is a path
    # In a path, there are exactly two vertices with degree 1, and the rest have degree 2
    count_degree_1 = sum(1 for d in degrees if d == 1)
    count_degree_2 = sum(1 for d in degrees if d == 2)
    count_degree_3_plus = sum(1 for d in degrees if d >= 3)
    
    # If exactly two vertices have degree 1 and all others have degree 2, it's a path
    if count_degree_1 == 2 and count_degree_2 == n - 2:
        print(1)
    elif count_degree_3_plus > 0:
        print(2)
    else:
        print(3)

solve()
```

```python
import sys
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build the graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n-1):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)
    
    # Count degrees
    degrees = [0] * n
    for i in range(n):
        degrees[i] = len(graph[i])
    
    # Check if the tree is a path
    count_degree_1 = sum(1 for d in degrees if d == 1)
    count_degree_2 = sum(1 for d in degrees if d == 2)
    
    # For a path, there should be exactly 2 vertices of degree 1
    if count_degree_1 == 2 and count_degree_2 == n - 2:
        print(1)
    else:
        print(2)

solve()
```

- 1: degree 1
- no vertex with degree >2, count=0, answer=1 → correct.

Example 3: as above, vertices 2 and 8 have degree >=3, so count=2, answer=3 → correct.

Therefore, the solution is to count the number of vertices with degree > 2 and add 1.

```python
n = int(input())
graph = {}

# Build the graph and degrees
degrees = [0] * n
for _ in range(n-1):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    degrees[a] += 1
    degrees[b] += 1

# Count vertices with degree > 2
count_high_degree = 0
for deg in degrees:
    if deg > 2:
        count_high_degree += 1

print(count_high_degree + 1)
```
